## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting shares | None — single class of ordinary shares, one vote per share; no preference shares or special voting rights disclosed | Capital IQ Historical Capitalization (Pref. Equity: nil across all periods); Capital IQ Public Ownership Summary (single share class) |
| Controlled-company status | De facto controlled by the Dubai government via Dubai Holding group (29.73%); no explicit formal "controlled-company" exemption disclosed (not applicable in UAE corporate governance framework as it is in US NYSE / NASDAQ rules) | Key Developments (May-11-2026 M&A closing); Capital IQ Public Ownership Summary |
| Shareholder bloc with board-nomination rights | Three of the ten current board members are clearly Dubai Holding group-linked (Omar Karim = Dubai Holding Group CIO; H.E. Abdullah Al-Falasi = UAE government official; H.E. Matar AlHemeiri = Digital Dubai government; H.E. Ahmed Bin Meshar = UAE government legal background). No formal written nomination agreement disclosed in available English-language filings; board composition is consistent with the government bloc exercising influence over director appointments | Capital IQ Board Members export (director profiles for Al-Falasi, AlHemeiri, Karim, Meshar, all added 2024–2025) |
| Founder's board position / veto | Mohamed Alabbar: Founder & MD, Executive & Non-Independent Director since 1997; no formal contractual veto rights identified; effective influence is reputational and relational rather than structural | Capital IQ Board Members (Alabbar profile) |

**Controlling-owner objective alignment (Filter 6 / CLAUDE.md §24):**

Emaar's 29.73% controlling bloc is a cluster of Dubai government-controlled investment vehicles (Emirates Power Investment LLC, Dubai Holding Corporate LLC, Eitl Difc Spc 1 Ltd). This is **government control**, one of the three structures that creates an inherent conflict with minority holders under CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6 and MODULE_RULES.md Score Cap Rules.

The conflict is structural, not hypothetical. Dubai's government pursues multiple objectives beyond per-share value maximisation: the Emaar platform serves as an instrument of urban policy (delivering master-planned communities, creating employment, developing Dubai's built environment), a credit for the city's branding (Downtown Dubai, Dubai Mall, Burj Khalifa), and a vehicle for selective land transactions between government entities. The AED 200 bn new masterplan announced on June 11, 2026 — with 4.5 million sqm GFA, 150,000 residents, no pre-sale gate, no phased targets — is consistent with a government agenda to create an iconic new Dubai district, regardless of whether the financial return to EMAR minority shareholders justifies committing this capital at a probable cycle peak.

Additional evidence of the government-owner priority conflict: (a) Emaar's most significant recent acquisition (Dubai Creek Harbour) was transacted with a government entity; (b) the RPT notes in the FY2024 annual report are available only in Arabic, limiting minority-shareholder ability to monitor government-counterparty terms; (c) three of the four board members added in 2024–2025 carry Dubai government backgrounds, deepening government influence at the board level over this period.

**This triggers RF-OWN-004.** The shareholder-friendliness score is capped at max 55 and the governance risk floor is set at 55 (per MODULE_RULES.md Score Cap Rules). Persistent cheapness under a government-aligned owner reflects the structural conflict, not a temporary mispricing to arbitrage. The valuation module should treat government control as a structural cap on the P/E and EV/EBITDA multiple Emaar can sustainably trade at versus a private-sector developer, not as a temporary margin of safety. This is a value-trap risk if the multiple gap persists without minority-shareholder-friendly catalysts.

**Emaar as a listed parent of listed subsidiaries:** Emaar also partially owns two listed subsidiaries — Emaar Development PJSC (DFM:EMAARDEV, ~80% owned) and Emaar Misr for Development (CASE:EMFD, ~73.79% owned). The structure means minority interests in these subsidiaries create earnings leakage (AED 4.7 bn of the group's AED 22.3 bn net income in FY2025, up 152% since FY2021). While Emaar itself is not a subsidiary of a parent maximising value elsewhere, the listed-subsidiary dynamic creates an opposite version of the conflict: the parent (EMAR) could theoretically extract value from the listed subsidiaries at minority-holder expense, and conversely the growing minority leakage means EMAR holders receive only ~79% of consolidated group earnings.

| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | None recorded in Key Developments (last 6 months) | Capital IQ Key Developments |

This is the most significant institutional holder exit in the available data period. **Market conduct flags:** No insider-selling-before-bad-news event is visible in the data pool. The Vanguard exit is a high-quality-holder departure that deserves monitoring but cannot be attributed to inside information with available evidence. No RF-MKT-001 or RF-MKT-002 is triggered — available pool data (a pool filing) shows no individual insider selling, and the government-transfer event (May 11, 2026) moved through a formal M&A closing announcement before it took effect, with no evidence of price dislocation beforehand.

---

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

The founder (Alabbar) holds a negligible economic stake (~0.03% of shares outstanding in the entire "Individuals/Insiders" category, which may include multiple individuals) — there is essentially no cash skin-in-the-game for individual insiders, and shares were not purchased by Alabbar in the open market at any point visible in the data pool. The alignment between Emaar management and minority shareholders rests entirely on the founder's reputational capital and the government bloc's long-term interest in Emaar's success as a vehicle for Dubai urban policy, not on meaningful personal financial alignment.

The net insider signal is **neutral** — no individual buys, no individual sells, with the qualification that the UAE disclosure regime makes it structurally impossible to observe individual insider activity. The most material ownership event of the past 12 months (ICD → Emirates Power Investment LLC transfer) was a government portfolio reorganisation that deepened Dubai Holding's grip on the company without injecting a single new outside shareholder.

The biggest control risk to minority holders is the government-owner's structural incentive to use Emaar as a city-development vehicle rather than a pure returns-maximising enterprise. The AED 200 bn masterplan, the opacity of RPT terms (Arabic-only FY2024 notes), the growing government presence on the board (three new government-linked directors added 2024–2025), and the absence of a formal minority-protection mechanism all point in the same direction: Emaar is a genuinely outstanding developer operating in a world-class asset base, but the government-control structure means minority holders bear development-cycle risk without the protection of a controlling owner whose sole interest is maximising per-share value.

## management-governance / 05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md

_Source: `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`_

# Board & Shareholder Rights — EMAR

**Ticker:** EMAR (Emaar Properties PJSC, DFM:EMAAR)
**Date:** 2026-07-03
**Jurisdiction:** UAE / Dubai Financial Market — filing regime under UAE Companies Law and UAE Securities and Commodities Authority (SCA) / DFM governance rules. No DEF 14A equivalent exists; the proxy-equivalent is the AGM Notice + the Corporate Governance Report (embedded in the Arabic Annual Report). CLAUDE.md §27 applied throughout.
**Accounting standard:** IFRS. **Currency:** AED. **Fiscal year:** January 1 – December 31.

**Cross-module inputs used:**
- `analyses/EMAR_2026-07-03/management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md` — upstream input
- `analyses/EMAR_2026-07-03/business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` — no disqualifier triggered
- `analyses/EMAR_2026-07-03/business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` — RPT, insider ownership signal
- `analyses/EMAR_2026-07-03/business-model/12_red-flags-sweep.md` — key-person risk, RPT, minority leakage

**Partial-data condition:**
The FY2024 Annual Report (the most recent audited filing) is in Arabic and was not English-extracted. The RPT note, Auditor's Report text, and the Corporate Governance Report (board committee terms, independence declarations, voting results) are in that document and cannot be fully assessed from available pool data. Quantitative RPT values are not recoverable. AGM voting outcomes are referenced by agenda but vote counts and dissent levels are not in the pool. This note is carried where it affects scoring.

---

## 1. Board Composition

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Board size | 10 members (9 current directors in the active board; Al Matrooshi listed as "Executive Director" without a current role; 9 functionally active) | Capital IQ Board Members export, data as of Jun-2026 |
| Independent directors (%) | 2 of 10 formally classified as "Non-Executive & Independent" (Eman Mahmood Ahmed Abdul Razzaq; Buti Obaid Buti Al Mulla) = 20%. Four additional directors are "Non-Executive & Non-Independent" (Bin Thaniah / Chairman; Ahmed Jamal Hassan Jawa / Vice Chairman; Al-Falasi; AlHemeiri). Three directors are "Internal" or otherwise non-independent (Alabbar / founder-MD; Al Matrooshi; Ali H. Odeh / internal unit CEO). | Capital IQ Board Members export, data as of Jun-2026 |
| Chair / CEO split? | Yes — formally split. Jamal Majed Khalfan Bin Thaniah is Non-Executive & Non-Independent Chairman (since Dec 2020); Amit Jain is Group CEO. However, Bin Thaniah is non-independent (Group CEO of Dubai World Corporation / Port & Free Zone World, a government-linked entity), so the split is formal rather than substantively independent. | Capital IQ Board Members export, data as of Jun-2026 |
| Audit committee independence | Not assessable from available data. The AGM agenda (Mar 25, 2026 Key Development) references the Corporate Governance Report section 3(c)(2) and "Appendix D" for board discharge and remuneration approval — both are in the Arabic Annual Report, not English-extracted. Ahmed Jamal Hassan Jawa (Vice Chairman, non-independent) is listed under "Member of Audit Committee" in Capital IQ. If the Audit Committee chair is non-independent, this is a governance deficiency. | Capital IQ Board Members (Jawa: "Member of Audit Committee"), AGM Notice Mar-25-2026 Key Development; FY2024 Arabic Annual Report (not English-extracted) |
| Compensation committee independence | Ahmed Jamal Hassan Jawa (non-independent) and Eman Abdul Razzaq (independent) both listed as members; Abdul Razzaq listed as "Chairman of Compensation Committee." | Capital IQ Board Members export, data as of Jun-2026 |
| Nominating committee independence | Ahmed Jamal Hassan Jawa (non-independent) and Eman Abdul Razzaq (independent) both listed under Nominating Committee; Abdul Razzaq listed as "Chairman of Nominating Committee." The Vice Chairman (non-independent) sitting on the nominating committee is a structural conflict, as nomination of independent directors is then co-chaired with a non-independent director. | Capital IQ Board Members export, data as of Jun-2026 |

**Assessment:** Only 20% of board seats are formally classified as independent — well below the 50%+ threshold that would be considered adequate for a company of Emaar's size and government-linked shareholder structure. The formal chair/CEO split is real but the Chairman is himself non-independent (government-linked entity). The two independent directors (Abdul Razzaq, Al Mulla) have meaningful professional credentials (Emirates NBD Group CHRO; Dubai Insurance Chairman) and sit on key committees, but they are structurally outvoted on any contested matter given 8 non-independent members. UAE SCA governance rules require independent directors on listed company boards, but the precise required percentage and the definition of independence under UAE law are not available in the pool; this assessment is based on the Capital IQ classification. Inference: the board's formal independent count likely meets UAE SCA minimums (UAE rules require at least one independent director) but falls well below global governance standards.

---

## 2. Tenure & Refreshment

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Average director tenure | Weighted by current members: Bin Thaniah — 14 years (DFM since 2012); Jawa — 20 years (since 2006); Alabbar — 29 years (since 1997 founding); Abdul Razzaq — 5 years (since 2021); Al Mulla — 5 years (since 2021); Al-Falasi — 2 years (since 2024); AlHemeiri — 1 year (since 2025); Karim — 2 years (since 2024); Meshar — 2 years (since 2024); Al Matrooshi — listed but departed 2024. Excluding Al Matrooshi: average tenure ~9 years for active directors. Three members (Al-Falasi, AlHemeiri, Karim, Meshar) joined within 2024–2025, which is meaningful refreshment. | Capital IQ Board Members export, data as of Jun-2026 |
| Overboarding / staleness signs | Ahmed Jamal Hassan Jawa (Vice Chairman): sits on boards of Emaar Development PJSC, RAK Petroleum, Starling International Management, Al Salam Bank Bahrain (prior). Multiple active commitments. Mohamed Alabbar (Founder/MD): simultaneously chairs Eagle Hills Properties LLC, Americana Restaurants International PLC, RSH Ltd., Emaar Misr, SUNCE HOTELI, and holds multiple other board memberships. Alabbar's overboarding is the most significant: he chairs a directly competing developer (Eagle Hills) while serving as Emaar's MD. This is a conflict of interest and a time-allocation concern, not merely a governance checklist item. Buti Al Mulla: sits on boards of Emirates NBD Bank PJSC, Emirates Islamic Bank PJSC, Dubai Insurance Company, Oman Refreshment Company, Dubai Refreshment — five current board seats, which may be at or above an overboarding threshold. | Capital IQ Board Members export ("Other Boards" column), data as of Jun-2026 |
| Recent refreshment | Four new directors added in 2024–2025 (Al-Falasi, Karim, Meshar in 2024; AlHemeiri in 2025); Ahmad Thani Al Matrooshi stepped down in 2024 after serving since 2005. Net refreshment of ~40% of seats in two years is positive for reducing staleness. However, the new 2024 directors include Omar Karim (Dubai Holding Group Chief Investment Officer) — a representative of the dominant government shareholder, not an independent appointment — and Al-Falasi and Meshar (both Dubai government career professionals), further concentrating government-linked representation rather than adding independent oversight. | Capital IQ Board Members export, data as of Jun-2026; 01_management-and-track-record.md |

**Assessment:** Two staleness issues coexist with genuine refreshment. Positive: four new directors in two years prevents a board that calcifies. Negative: the long-tenured non-independent directors (Jawa at 20 years, Alabbar at 29 years) have been in place so long that questioning management's strategy becomes structurally unlikely. The three new 2024 directors who are government-affiliated increase the concentration of government-aligned representation. Alabbar's concurrent chairmanship of Eagle Hills (a competing developer) is the single most material overboarding/conflict-of-interest issue — it is unresolved in any English-language disclosure.

---

## 3. Related-Party Transactions & Group Leakage

**Data limitation:** Quantitative RPT data from the FY2024 audited annual report is not recoverable because the relevant notes are in Arabic and were not English-extracted. The FY2025 audited annual report has not yet been filed (FY2025 financials are in preliminary/investor-presentation form only). No English-language RPT note is available in this pool. The table below uses the best available evidence and marks amounts "Not assessable" where the data pool does not support a figure.

**Key RPT observations:**

The absence of an English-language RPT note is the single largest governance data gap in this pool. However, three structural RPT-risk channels are visible from English disclosures:

1. **Government-counterparty channel.** The dominant shareholder (Dubai Holding / Emirates Power Investment LLC, 29.73%) is a government entity, and Emaar's largest recent acquisition (Dubai Creek Harbour, AED 7.5 bn, FY2022) was from a government-related entity (Dubai Holding). Whether this was done at arm's length — i.e., market price — is not proven from available data. If it was priced at a discount to independent appraisal, this is value leakage from minority holders to government. The FY2022 acquisition price of AED 7.5 bn for Dubai Creek Harbour is a material data point: the land bank sits in Emaar's balance sheet at acquisition cost, and future development profits belong to all shareholders including minorities. The concern is on the acquisition side: overpaying for government assets would reduce returns.

2. **Alabbar conflict-of-interest channel.** Mohamed Alabbar simultaneously chairs Eagle Hills Properties LLC, a private developer directly competing in UAE and regional real estate and hospitality markets. Eagle Hills acquired Four Seasons Hotel Marrakech on Jan 30, 2026, competing with Emaar's hospitality strategy. No English-language disclosure addresses whether Emaar has any commercial transactions with Eagle Hills or Alabbar-affiliated entities, or whether conflict-of-interest procedures are in place. Until an English RPT note is available, this cannot be quantified or cleared.

3. **Minority leakage through listed subsidiaries.** Emaar's minority interest (non-controlling interest) has grown from AED 1.9 bn (FY2021) to AED 4.7 bn (FY2025), a 152% increase. While this is structurally driven by the listed subsidiary structure (Emaar Development PJSC and Emaar Misr are both independently listed), EMAR common shareholders capture only ~79% of consolidated net income. This is not classical RPT leakage but it is a structural dilution of economics for EMAR holders that grows with the subsidiary earnings.

The disqualifier scan did not find RPTs above 25% of revenue/expenses — the hard disqualifier threshold. No RF-RPT-001 is triggered at this time. However, the data gap prevents confirmation of arm's-length terms on government-counterparty transactions or Alabbar-linked transactions. An Amber flag applies pending English-language disclosure.

**Additional voting context:** The Dubai Holding bloc (29.73%) and Eitl Difc Spc 1 Ltd (7.46%) together represent 37.19% of shares. If institutions (18.45%) are split, the government-linked bloc + Eitl effectively control most ordinary resolutions requiring simple majority. Contested resolutions against management's recommended slate are unlikely to pass without institutional coordination, given that the largest single identifiable bloc is government-aligned and management-supporting. Institutional holders (Vanguard 2.70%, BlackRock 2.52%) have the scale to signal dissent but not to block resolutions independently.

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

The board is **not a genuine check on management or the controlling government shareholder.** With only 20% formally independent directors (2 of 10), a non-independent Chairman, a non-independent director sitting on both the Audit and Nominating Committees, and three new 2024 directors who are government-career professionals nominated by or aligned with the dominant 29.73% government bloc, the board's structural function is to ratify management decisions rather than challenge them. The two genuinely independent directors (Abdul Razzaq, Al Mulla) chair the Compensation and Nominating Committees — a positive signal — but are structurally outvoted on any contested matter and have no disclosed record of dissent or opposing management on any issue in the available data.

The single biggest entrenchment risk to minority holders is the combination of (a) Mohamed Alabbar's dual role as Emaar's MD and simultaneous chairman of Eagle Hills Properties LLC, a direct competitor, with no disclosed conflict-of-interest procedure in English, and (b) the government shareholder bloc's ability to approve or block any ordinary resolution without minority input, in a company where related-party pricing on government-land transactions (including the AED 7.5 bn Dubai Creek Harbour acquisition in FY2022) is not independently verifiable from available English-language disclosures.

Shareholder rights are structurally weak: no poison pill (a positive for takeover protection but irrelevant given government dominance), no staggered board, no dual-class structure (formally clean), but no mechanism for minority shareholders to meaningfully contest management decisions or force disclosure of RPT terms. AGM outcomes are not publicly disclosed in dissent-detail format. Written consent and EGM calling rights are limited under UAE law. Per MODULE_RULES.md §24 Filter 6: the dominant 29.73% owner is a government-controlled entity (Dubai Holding / Emirates Power Investment LLC) whose objective is not necessarily long-run per-share value maximisation for Emaar minorities — it is the broader development agenda of the Dubai government. This structural misalignment between the controlling owner's goal and minority-shareholder interests permanently caps the minority-protection read.

**Verdict: weak / entrenched.** The government-controlled bloc, lack of board independence, unresolved Alabbar conflict of interest, and unavailability of RPT terms in English combine to create structural minority-shareholder exposure. No critical red flag (RPT breach, audit qualification, enforcement action) is proven from available data — but the architecture is not one where minority holders can rely on the board as an independent agent. Applying §24 Filter 6: the controlling owner is a government entity; the board cannot offset a structurally misaligned owner; minority protection is capped at "weak / entrenched" on this basis alone, independent of the other findings.

Score: **34 / 100 — Weak.** Falls in the 21–40 band ("Weak / high risk" per MODULE_RULES.md scoring). The low score reflects: (a) formally very low board independence (20%), (b) unresolved Alabbar conflict of interest, (c) government-aligned anchor bloc, (d) RPT data gap, and (e) §24 Filter 6 structural cap applied. Two score caps are applied from MODULE_RULES.md Score Cap Rules: **Shareholder friendliness max 55** (government-controlled owner per §24 Filter 6) and **Governance risk floor 55** (same trigger). The raw component scores are already within or below these caps, so the caps do not further reduce the total here — but they constrain any upgrade from new information unless the ownership structure changes.

**Source conflicts:** The Capital IQ Board Members export lists both Ahmed Jamal Hassan Jawa and Eman Abdul Razzaq as "Chairman of Nominating Committee." This is likely a data artifact in the CIQ export (both may be listed as having held the chairmanship at different points, or the committee has co-chairs). The field listing Jawa under "Member of Audit Committee" may be outdated if he has since rotated off. These CIQ-export ambiguities lower confidence in precise committee composition to 3/5. The Arabic Annual Report would be the authoritative source.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — EMAR

**Ticker:** EMAR (Emaar Properties PJSC, DFM)
**Date:** 2026-07-03
**Reporting standard:** IFRS | **Currency:** AED | **Fiscal year:** 31 December
**Jurisdiction:** UAE / DFM — disclosure regime is UAE SCA / ESCA and DFM Listing Rules (not SEC)
**Partial data note:** No earnings call transcripts are in the data pool. Q&A transparency cannot be assessed. The Q&A transparency component is marked "Insufficient Data" and the Disclosure Candor Score is capped accordingly (max 85 on total, Q&A marked N/A). The `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md` cross-check is available and used throughout.

---

## 1. Owning the Misses

The one material delivery failure identified in `01_management-and-track-record.md` is Emaar Misr's income collapse in FY2025. The table below assesses how it was communicated.

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| International Development EBITDA margin collapsed from 96% (FY2024) to 31% (FY2025); Emaar Misr net profit fell 65% in AED terms and 65% in EGP terms in one year while revenue was roughly flat (+5% in EGP) | The FY2025 investor presentation (Slide 21) disclosed the numbers in a table showing FY2025 vs FY2024 comparisons with clear "% Change" columns. However, the slide's header banner read "Emaar Misr Positioned itself as Local Market Leader, Delivering Superior Growth." The bullet-point "Key Highlights" section led with: "Achieved Net Sales of AED 6.8 Bn in 2025, over 4x of net sales of 2024." The one partial explanation offered was that "2024 EBITDA includes forex gain of AED ~650 Mn vs forex loss of AED ~170 Mn in 2025" — a note that explains only a portion of the AED 1,080 Mn EBITDA decline (AED 1,559 Mn → AED 479 Mn, a drop of AED 1,080 Mn). The residual decline (~AED 430 Mn after adjusting for the ~AED 820 Mn combined FX swing) remains unexplained in the available English disclosures. The CEO/Alabbar quote in the press release made no mention of the international segment deterioration. No prior-period filing (FY2023 press release, Feb 8, 2024) carried any risk warning about Egyptian margin pressure — it described Egypt operations positively as one of the drivers of international performance. | Partially obscured. The numbers are in the table and cannot be hidden; the partial FX explanation is useful. But the framing — promotional slide header, bullet leading with sales growth rather than profitability deterioration, no CEO acknowledgment of the miss — buries a significant income collapse under positive spin. The income collapse is not "owned" in the sense of being foregrounded, explained in full, or addressed by name by management as a problem requiring remediation. RF-DISC-001 from `01` stands. | FY2025 Investor Presentation, Feb-12-2026, Slide 21; FY2023 Preliminary Annual Report press release, Feb-08-2024, "Emaar International" section; Capital IQ Key Developments, Emaar Misr earnings announcement, Feb-23-2026 |
| FY2022 EPS miss of −10.8% vs consensus (the one recent annual miss) | Not addressed in the FY2022 press release (Feb 14, 2023). The tone was entirely backward-looking positive: "record property sales of AED 35.1 billion," "EBITDA increased by 18%." The EPS miss vs consensus was a Street expectation miss, not a company-vs-guidance miss (Emaar issues no guidance), so the company had no obligation to address it. The framing of the FY2022 release was positive throughout with no acknowledgment that the Dubai property market had softened from its COVID-rebound peak. | Neutral — Emaar does not issue guidance so cannot "miss" its own target. The consensus miss is an analyst expectation issue. No obscuring, but equally no acknowledgment. | FY2022 Preliminary Annual Report press release, Feb-14-2023 |
| CFO/Group Head of Finance (Hesham Heikal) departure, May 2026, without disclosed reason | Announced via DFM Key Development disclosure; no reason given. Internally described as a succession (Chindalia in from Emaar Development). The absence of a stated reason for a C-suite departure is not unusual in the UAE governance regime, but it removes accountability context in a period of Emaar Misr margin stress. | Obscured by omission — reason not disclosed. Not a reporting requirement in UAE SCA rules, but a credibility gap nonetheless. | Capital IQ Key Developments, May 22, 2026 |
| AED 200 bn masterplan announced with no phased targets, milestones, or pre-sale thresholds (June 11, 2026) | Announced as a "product-related announcement" without any investment breakdown, construction-start timeline, pre-sale gating conditions, or capital commitment schedule. No detail on land acquisition cost or phasing has been disclosed in any available English filing as of July 3, 2026. | Obscured — the absence of detail on a commitment of this scale is itself a disclosure failure. Management is asking investors to credit a plan of unprecedented scope without providing the numbers that would allow any accountability check. | Capital IQ Key Developments, Jun-11-2026 |

---

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

Cross-referenced against `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md`, which assigned an earnings quality score of 72/100 (mostly clean). Assessment below is on the candor dimension of those adjustments.

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | Asset writedowns appeared in FY2021 (AED 667 Mn), FY2022 (AED 916 Mn), and FY2023 (AED 2,011 Mn) — three consecutive years, totalling AED 3,594 Mn. They were absent in FY2024–FY2025. These items are real economic losses but their recurring nature means they were, in practice, a structural cost of the development cycle during that period, not one-off events. Their classification as adjustable items (they inflate reported CFO by adding back non-cash losses) in the same period every year is a mild candor concern. However, they stopped in FY2024–FY2025 — consistent with genuine portfolio cleanup rather than ongoing manipulation. | earnings/06_earnings-quality.md, Section 5 |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | The single material recurring adjustment is Company EBITDA vs reported EBITDA: FY2025 gap of +AED 1,429 Mn (+5.9% above reported) and FY2024 gap of +AED 1,714 Mn (+9.8%). This adjusts for the non-cash IFRS 9 discounting unwind on long-term receivables — a mechanically correct and clearly disclosed adjustment. At 5.9%–9.8% of reported EBITDA it is not immaterial but it is not aggressive. The adjustment is consistently disclosed in the investor presentation's "Key Financial Highlights" section with the exact line-item explanation. | earnings/06_earnings-quality.md, Section 7; FY2025 Investor Presentation, Feb-12-2026, Slide 12 (Key Financial Highlights) |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Modestly, in two specific ways. (1) Net Cash is disclosed as AED 61,655 Mn (FY2025), defined as Cash + Short-Term Deposits minus Debt. This "broad" basis — netting in AED 18,828 Mn of short-term deposits alongside AED 52,633 Mn of unrestricted cash equivalents — gives a substantially higher headline than the strict net cash figure (Cash − Debt = AED 52,633 Mn − AED 9,806 Mn = AED 42,827 Mn), or the truly free cash figure after removing AED ~43 Bn of project escrow balances (strict net cash outside escrow: AED 17,639 Mn per `business-model/11`). Management presents AED 61.7 Bn on Slide 8 without a basis label. (2) Headline CFO and FCF are inflated by off-plan buyer advance payments (AED 8,229 Mn in FY2025). This is disclosed in the cash flow statement and noted in the investor presentation (Slide 14 footnote), but the headline FCF figure is not separately presented on a normalised basis anywhere in the deck. The promotional language on Slide 9 ("compelling investment opportunity at significant discount," "exceptional financial strength," "fortress balance sheet") reflects genuine outperformance but is framed at the upper limit of supportable promotional language. | FY2025 Investor Presentation, Feb-12-2026, Slides 8, 14; earnings/06_earnings-quality.md, Sections 1, 7 |

**Overall non-GAAP verdict: conservative to neutral.** The one recurring adjustment is mechanically sound and disclosed. The writedown pattern FY2021–FY2023 is a minor historical flag. The net cash definition and headline FCF require investor interpretation but are not manipulative. This is a higher-quality disclosure posture than most regional peers on the adjustments dimension.

---

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Emaar provides good structural segment disclosure: four operating segments (UAE Development, International Development, Emaar Malls, Emaar Hospitality) each reported with revenue, gross profit, EBITDA, and net profit in its preliminary annual reports, and with KPIs (units sold, units delivered, backlog, occupancy, WALT) that are consistent period-over-period. The UAE Development slide is particularly detailed — project-level disclosure (Slide 19 of the FY2025 deck shows each development's units, area, % sold, % collected, gross margin achieved, and remaining GFA by type). This level of granularity is above average for the sector.

The two disclosure gaps that matter most are: (a) the English-language audited annual report is not yet filed for FY2025 as of the analysis date (July 3, 2026), and the FY2024 audited annual report (filed March 14, 2025) is in Arabic only in the data pool — meaning English-language investors have access to preliminary (unaudited) results and investor presentations but not to audited notes, related-party transaction disclosures, accounting policy changes, or auditor communications in English; (b) the RPT note and details of Alabbar's Eagle Hills relationship are accessible only in Arabic, making an English-language conflict-of-interest read impossible from available pool sources. These are not retrograde changes — the regime has been the same throughout — but they represent a structural gap in accessibility. There is no evidence that disclosure has become less transparent over time; if anything, the KPI set in the deck has expanded. However, the partial-English-disclosure structure means Emaar's de facto English disclosure is less complete than an SEC-registered or fully bilingual issuer's disclosure would be.

---

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

The FY2025 preliminary annual report (the worst period for the international segment, released Feb 12, 2026) is the right test. The cover slide reads "Prepared for the Future — Built on Strength, Prudence & Resilience." The consolidated results slide leads with "2025: Another record year with highest ever Sales, Revenue and Profitability." The Alabbar quote in the Q1 2025 press release (April 8, 2026, Arabic-language version) emphasised "new standards of operational excellence" and "sustainable momentum." No English-language CEO/MD statement in any filing specifically addressed Emaar Misr's margin collapse, the unexplained CFO departure, or the risks attached to the AED 200 bn masterplan launch at a cycle peak. The tone is uniformly promotional across every period reviewed — the FY2022 press release (Feb 14, 2023, a year of EPS underperformance vs estimates) was similarly positioned around record property sales, with Alabbar's quote closing on a forward-looking positive commitment. No earnings call transcripts are available in the data pool, so Q&A behaviour under analyst questioning cannot be assessed. Management appears candid in the sense that numbers are reported without manipulation; but the narrative framing in the worst period actively foregrounded positives and marginalised the one significant operational failure.

---

## 5. Candor Read

Emaar's financial numbers are reported honestly — the IFRS cash flow statement discloses buyer advance payments, the investor presentation discloses the EBITDA adjustment and provides project-level detail, and no evidence of accounting manipulation is present. On that narrow definition, management is reporting truthfully. But the higher standard for candor — owning misses, foregrounding problems, giving investors the information they need to monitor what went wrong — is not met. The Emaar Misr income collapse (EBITDA −69%, net profit −65% in AED terms in one year) was disclosed in a table on one slide, immediately followed by a banner reading "Local Market Leader, Delivering Superior Growth," with bullets emphasising sales growth and backlog size. No management quote anywhere in the English-language data pool addressed this collapse, explained its cause, or committed to a recovery path. The AED 200 bn masterplan announcement offers no phased targets, no pre-sale gates, and no cost-commitment schedule — management is asking investors to trust a plan of unprecedented scale with no checkable evidence. These two patterns — burying a large operational failure under promotional framing, and announcing a major commitment without accountability detail — are the clearest candor signals in this dataset. The absence of formal guidance also structurally limits accountability: with no numerical forward commitments, there is nothing specific to own when outcomes disappoint. The one area where management is clearly above average on transparency is structural segment disclosure and KPI consistency, which protects the verdict from a "low-trust" rating.

**Verdict: mixed.** Management discloses numbers without manipulation and provides above-average structural transparency, but it does not own operational misses, uses persistently promotional tone even in adverse periods, and announces major commitments without any accountability detail.

*Note: Q&A transparency (15 pts) is marked Insufficient Data due to absence of transcripts. The component is scored 0 with partial-data notation, reducing the achievable maximum from 100 to 85 on an adjusted basis. The total is presented as scored / 100 with the cap noted.*

| Component | Score | Max Score | Evidence |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Owning misses | 13 | 25 | Emaar Misr collapse disclosed in table but buried under promotional framing; no CEO/MD quote owns it; no prior warning; CFO departure reason withheld; AED 200 bn masterplan lacks accountability detail. Partial credit for the table disclosure and partial FX explanation. |
| KPI consistency | 17 | 20 | Four-segment structure, consistent KPIs period-over-period, project-level detail on UAE Development (Slide 19). One flag: reclassification of "Malls JV assets" into "Emaar Malls" segment in FY2025 (noted in Slide 13 footnote). Not material enough to penalise heavily. |
| Non-GAAP / adjustment discipline | 15 | 20 | Single recurring adjustment (IFRS 9/15) is sound and disclosed; no SBC exclusion; recurring writedowns FY2021–FY2023 are a minor historical flag; net cash definition uses broad basis without headline label (−3 pts); no recurring "adjusted EPS" that materially inflates the picture. |
| Q&A transparency | 0 | 15 | Insufficient Data — no transcripts in pool. Score capped at 0; cap applied (see note above). |
| Disclosure timeliness | 9 | 10 | Preliminary results filed within 43 days of FY2025 year-end; consistent with DFM schedule. −1 for FY2024 audited annual report being in Arabic only (accessibility gap). |
| Specificity vs boilerplate | 6 | 10 | UAE Development disclosures are highly specific (project-level table, margins, % collected). International Development and Emaar Misr disclosures are minimally specific — one summary slide with partial FX note. CEO/MD quotes are pure boilerplate ("enduring commitment," "unparalleled offerings") with no specific candid observation. AED 200 bn masterplan announcement is entirely unspecific. |
| **Total** | **60** | **100** | Partial-data cap applied: Q&A component scored 0 (Insufficient Data). Effective achievable maximum with available data is 85 pts; score of 60/85 = 71% on available data, 60/100 on full scale. |
